BMS Modular Diaries: Torus one-point function
Arjun Bagchi, Poulami Nandi, Amartya Saha, and Zodinmawia

Abstract
Two dimensional field theories invariant under the Bondi-Metzner-Sachs (BMS) group are conjectured to be dual to asymptotically flat spacetimes in three dimensions. In this paper, we continue our investigations of the modular properties of these field theories. In particular, we focus on the BMS torus one-point function. We use two different methods to arrive at expressions for asymptotic structure constants for general states in the theory utilising modular properties of the torus one-point function. We then concentrate on the BMS highest weight representation, and derive a host of new results, the most important of which is the BMS torus block. In a particular limit of large weights, we derive the leading and sub-leading pieces of the BMS torus block, which we then use to rederive an expression for the asymptotic structure constants for BMS primaries. Finally, we perform a bulk computation of a probe scalar in the background of a flatspace cosmological solution based on the geodesic approximation to reproduce our field theoretic results.



1
Introduction

1.1
Preliminaries: Conformal symmetry

The techniques of conformal field theory (CFT)
DiFrancesco:1997nk
;
Blumenhagen:2009zz
have been central to our understanding of various diverse subjects including the theory of phase transitions in statistical physics, the understanding of string theory from worldsheet symmetries, the study of quantum gravity in AdS (and dS) spacetimes through the celebrated gauge-gravity correspondence, as well as explorations in cosmology. These techniques hinge on the extra symmetries CFTs possess vis-a-vis usual relativistic quantum field theories (QFTs). Of particular importance is the procedure of conformal bootstrap
Ferrara:1973yt
;
Polyakov:1974gs
;
Poland:2016chs
;
Simmons-Duffin:2016gjk
, which aims to solve CFTs by imposing an infinite set of consistency conditions on the theory that stem from crossing symmetry of the four-point function. This programme has seen a resurgence over the last decade, following the seminal work
Rattazzi:2008pe
.

In general
d
dimensions, the symmetry algebra that underlies a CFT is
so(d,2)
as opposed to
iso(d-1,1)
of a Poincare-invariant QFT in the same dimension. In two dimensional CFTs, the underlying symmetry enhances to two copies of the infinite dimensional Virasoro algebra. Following the lead of Belavin, Polyakov and Zamolodchikov
Belavin:1984vu
, over the years, 2d CFTs have been the most fertile of all theories for performing analytic calculations. The power of infinite symmetry coupled with the might of the machinery of complex analysis has helped with an enormous amount of analytic control on calculations in 2d CFTs in general, without having to refer to any particular Lagrangian description. Before the current resurgence mentioned above, conformal bootstrap techniques were used in 2d to exactly solve a class of CFTs, called the 2d Minimal models.

In addition to consistency from crossing symmetry of 4-point function, in 2d CFTs there are further constraints that arise from modular invariance on the torus. These help in constraining the possible parameter space in 2d CFTs even further. Modular invariance has the additional very useful feature of relating the low energy spectrum of the theory to the high energy spectrum. This leads to the famous Cardy formula for entropy counting in 2d CFTs
Cardy:1986ie
, which spectacularly matches up with the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy of Banados-Teitelboim-Zanelli black holes in AdS
3
, and serves as one of the very early successes of the holographic principle
Strominger:1997eq
;
Carlip:1998qw
.

1.2
Flat spacetimes, BMS and Holography

Holography for AdS
3
uses the power of the infinite dimensional Virasoro symmetries, which are also the asymptotic symmetries of the bulk spacetime
Brown:1986nw
. However, infinite asymptotic symmetries are not exclusive to AdS
3
. Bondi, van der Burg, Mezner and, independently, Sachs in the 1960’s found rather surprisingly that the symmetries of 4d asymptotically flat spacetimes at null infinity (
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
) enhances from the expected Poincare group to to an infinite dimensional group, named BMS after the discoverers
Bondi:1962px
;
Sachs
. There has been a recent explosion in activity in research connecting the infrared physics of gauge theories and gravity to the BMS group
Strominger:2017zoo
. The existence of an infinite dimensional asymptotic symmetry algebra is also true in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes and the symmetry algebra in question is the BMS
3

Barnich:2006av
:

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+c_{L}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(1a)

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}+c_{M}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(1b)

\displaystyle[M_{n},M_{m}]=0.

(1c)

Here
L_{n}
’s are the so-called super-rotations, which are the diffeomorphisms of the circle at infinity.
M_{n}
’s are called super-translations and these are angle dependent translations along the null directions of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.
c_{L},c_{M}
are two allowed central extensions of the theory. For Einstein gravity,
c_{L}=0,c_{M}=1/4G
, where
G
is the Newton’s constant. One can generate other values of the central term by considering more general theories of gravity e.g. by adding gravitational Chern-Simons term to the Einstein-Hilbert action
Bagchi:2012yk
.

Figure 1:
Penrose diagram for three-dimensional Minkowski spacetime.

It is natural, following lessons of AdS
3
, to attempt constructions of holography in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes, using the algebra (
1
). Specifically, the putative dual 2d field theory should be one which lives on the null boundary, say
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, and be invariant under the symmetry algebra (
1
)
Bagchi:2010zz
;
Bagchi:2012cy
. As we will go on to describe in the next section, the BMS
3
algebra can be obtained by an Inönü-Wigner contraction of two copies of the Virasoro algebra. This contraction which is the infinite radius limit of AdS, manifests itself on the boundary theory as an ultra-relativistic or a Carrollian limit on the parent 2d CFT, where the speed of light goes to zero. This rather strange limit results in the degeneration of the background (flat) metric that the field theory lives on and the Riemannian structures are replaced by Carrollian structures. These Carrollian manifolds are generically fibre-bundles. In the case of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, the structure is of course a direct product
{\rm I\!R}_{u}\times S^{1}
, where
u
is the null direction. This is a flat Carrollian manifold. We would be interested in 2d conformal field theories defined on these manifolds, the so called conformal Carrollian theories. Conformal Carrollian symmetries have been shown to be isomorphic to BMS symmetries
Duval:2014uva
.

In this paper, continuing with our earlier endeavours, we take forward our construction of the modular properties of BMS-invariant field theories (henceforth abbreviated as BMSFTs)
Bagchi:2012xr
;
Bagchi:2013qva
;
Bagchi:2019unf
. As mentioned earlier, the BMS algebra can be arrived at from a limit of (two copies of) the Virasoro algebra. Hence it is conceivable that in this limit, some of the modular properties of 2d CFTs are also inherited by 2d BMSFTs. Following this line of argument, a BMS-Cardy formula
Bagchi:2012xr
was proposed using these contracted modular properties
1
1

1

See
Barnich:2012xq
for an alternative derivation.
. BTZ black holes are orbifolds of AdS
3
. Similar orbifolds exist for 3d Minkowski spacetimes. The so-called shifted boost orbifold turns out to be the analogue of the non-extremal BTZ and is a cosmological solution with a cosmological horizon
Cornalba:2003kd
. The thermodynamics of these cosmologies, also called Flat Space Cosmologies (FSCs) have been studied
Bagchi:2012xr
;
Barnich:2012xq
;
Bagchi:2013lma
;
Detournay:2014fva
and in particular, the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy of these FSCs have been reproduced by the BMS-Cardy formula adding the significant feather to the cap of the programme of construction of 3d flat holography
Bagchi:2012xr
. We will review modular properties and the BMS-Cardy formula in Sec. 2, and the FSC story briefly in Sec. (
8
).

Some other interesting advances in the study of holography for three dimensional asymptotic flat spacetimes include the construction of the flat limit of Liouville theory as an explicit putative boundary theory
Barnich:2012rz
, matching of correlation functions between bulk and boundary for stress-energy tensors
Bagchi:2015wna
and generic fields
Hijano:2017eii
, aspects of entanglement entropy
Bagchi:2014iea
;
Basu:2015evh
;
Hijano:2017eii
;
Jiang:2017ecm
;
Grumiller:2019xna
;
Fareghbal:2019czx
;
Godet:2019wje
;
Apolo:2020bld
;
Apolo:2020qjm
, understanding holographic reconstruction
Hartong:2015usd
, and the flat version of the fluid-gravity correspondence
Ciambelli:2018wre
.

1.3
Goal and summary of results of current work

The conformal bootstrap programme has been instrumental in constraining the landscape of known CFTs, and thereby relativistic QFT as renormalisation group flows away from CFTs, and ultimately aims to chart out all know and consistent relativistic theories. The main idea of the bootstrap is simple: any CFT is completely fixed by the dimension of primary operators and the structure constants characterising the three-point functions of primary operators. In 2d CFTs, an arbitrary correlation function or the partition function on an arbitrary Riemann surface can be completely fixed by this data. In two dimensions, imposing crossing symmetry of four-point functions on the sphere and modular covariance of one-point function on the torus implies the imposition of crossing and modular invariance to higher points and higher genus
Moore:1988uz
;
Sonoda:1988fq
. Hence investigation of properties of the torus one-point function is an important programme, which was initiated recently by Kraus and Maloney in
Kraus:2016nwo
. This has interesting holographic ramifications as well. Like the Cardy formula, the asymptotic behaviour of the structure constants or three-point coefficients of the theory is again fixed by the low lying spectrum. From the dual bulk, the field theory structure constants match with the corresponding expectation values in the dual BTZ geometry
Kraus:2016nwo
. See
Alkalaev:2016ptm
for a different slightly different bulk interpretation.

The BMS bootstrap programme has been initiated in
Bagchi:2016geg
and elaborated in
Bagchi:2017cpu
, together with other relevant developments
Hijano:2017eii
;
Hijano:2018nhq
;
Lodato:2018gyp
;
Merbis:2019wgk
, in an effort to constrain possible 2d BMSFTs. We wish to take this programme of constraining BMSFTs further by focusing on modular invariance. Following
Kraus:2016nwo
, the analysis of the torus one-point function has been generalised in many contexts, viz. to charged 2d CFTs
Das:2017vej
, warped CFTs
Song:2019txa
. In this paper, we work out the generalisation of the Kraus-Maloney analysis to the case of 2d BMS invariant field theories. In the process, we develop a lot of new machinery for BMSFTs, in particular derive expressions for the torus blocks of BMSFTs. Finally, we also show that the our expressions can be reproduced by looking at a one-point function in the putative dual FSC solution.

1.4
Outline of the paper and a quick look at the main results

Our paper is organised as follows. In Sec. (
2
), we review properties of 2d BMSFTs including in particular modular invariance of BMSFTs. In Sec (
3
), we provide a definition of the BMS torus one-point function and derive its modular properties.

In Sec (
4
), we discuss the derivation of the asymptotic form of the BMS structure constants by exploiting the modular properties of the torus one-point function. Here we employ two methods, one a saddle-point analysis and the second based on inverting integral transforms that improves on the saddle point analysis. These methods don’t assume much about the representations the states fall into in the sums used in the definition of the torus one-point function. Our principal result in this section is

\displaystyle C_{ipi}\approx D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})C_{\chi p\chi}\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta_{i}\xi_{p}}{2\xi_{i}}\right)\exp\left(-2\pi\frac{\Delta_{i}\xi_{\chi}}{\sqrt{2c_{M}\xi_{i}}}\right).

(2)

This above result is the outcome of the saddle point approximation and here presented for the case
c_{L}=0
. Here
C_{ipi}\equiv\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
is the three point-coefficient, where
\phi_{p}
is a BMS primary with weights
\Delta_{p}
and
\xi_{p}
.
\phi_{\chi}
is the lightest BMS primary in the spectrum above the vacuum and has weights
\Delta_{\chi}
and
\xi_{\chi}
, and
D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})
is its degeneracy.

There are further improvements on this result in Sec (
4
) to include a non-zero
c_{L}
, to increase the range of validity of the result and to include generalisations when the vacuum has a degeneracy as well as where the vacuum is not the lightest primary. We will give details of this in the main body of the paper.

In the latter half of the paper, we refine our analysis to look at the torus one-point function of BMS primaries in the highest weight representation. This entails the development of a lot of new results in the context of BMS highest weight representations in Sec. (
5
). Our primary achievement in this part of the paper outlined in Sec (
6
) which details the construction of the BMS torus blocks. These are defined by

\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho)=\frac{q^{-\Delta_{A}+\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{-\xi_{A}+\frac{c_{M}}{2}}}{C_{ApA}}{\rm Tr}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)q^{L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}\right).

(3)

Here the trace is over
\{A\}
, the collection of all the the primary states with dimension
(\Delta_{A},\xi_{A})
. Also
q=e^{2\pi i\sigma},\ y=e^{2\pi i\rho}
are the exponentiation of the BMS modular parameters
\sigma,\rho
. We present expressions of the BMS torus blocks in a certain limit of large BMS weights (large
\xi_{A}
) for these states. In this approximation, as detailed in the main body of the paper, the result for the BMS torus block reads:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho)\equiv\sum_{N}q^{N}\mathcal{F}_{N}(\Delta_{p},\xi_{p};\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|c_{L},c_{M}|\rho),\quad\text{where}

(4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}=\left(1+\frac{\xi_{p}(\Delta_{p}-1)}{2\xi_{A}}N\right)\widetilde{\text{dim}}_{N}+\pi i\rho\frac{\xi_{p}^{2}}{\xi_{A}}\sum_{k=0}^{N}p(N-k)p(k)(N-k)(N-2k)+\mathcal{O}(\xi_{A}^{-2}).

In the above,
p(N)
is the partition of the integer
N
and
\widetilde{\text{dim}}_{N}
is the partition of the same integer
N
using two colors. As an aside, using a differential equation arising from the quadratic Casimirs of the global BMS algebra (or the Poincare algebra), we also derive a closed form expression for the BMS global torus block.

Using all of these tools, in Sec (
7
), we arrive at the asymptotic structure constants for the BMS highest weight primaries. The form of this primary structure constant is almost identical to the one obtained in the analysis for general states in the earlier section with the identification

c_{L}\to c_{L}-\frac{1}{6}

(5)

This indicates a quantum shift in the central term
c_{L}
and no such correction in
c_{M}
, in keeping with recent literature
Merbis:2019wgk
. We have some more remarks at the end of Sec (
7
).

Sec. (
8
) contains a bulk computation that matches our field theory results, specifically (
2
). Here we first review the Flat Space Cosmology (FSC) solutions and then use a geodesic approximation to compute a probe one-point function in this FSC background. We end in Sec. (
9
) with some discussions and future directions. Complementing the analysis in the main text, there are twelve (no, really twelve!) appendices which outline various detailed computations omitted in the main body of the paper.

2
BMS field theories and modular invariance

In this section, we review aspects of BMS invariant field theories in 2d, or in short 2d BMSFTs. We start with algebraic preliminaries and then look at aspects of representation theory. We then discuss modular invariance in 2d BMSFTs. Since this is basically all review material, readers familiar with earlier work on BMSFT can skip over to the next section.

2.1
Algebra and contraction

As we have stated in the introduction, we will be interested in 2d quantum field theories invariant under the BMS
3
algebra, rewritten here for convenience:

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+c_{L}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(6a)

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}+c_{M}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(6b)

\displaystyle[M_{n},M_{m}]=0.

(6c)

This algebra (
6
) can be obtained from two copies of the Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(7a)

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=(n-m)\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n+m}+\frac{\bar{c}}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}

(7b)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=0.

(7c)

by an Inönü-Wigner contraction defined by

L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n},\quad M_{n}=\epsilon\left(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0.

(8)

We shall refer to this limit as the ultra-relativistic (UR) or Carrollian limit. In terms of coordinates, this has the interpretation of sending the speed of light to infinity in the dual field theory. We shall see this shortly. From the point of the bulk, the limit (
8
) can be identified as taking the radius of AdS (
\ell
) to infinity. This can be easily seen by considering the identification
\epsilon=1/\ell
. Remembering that the Brown-Henneaux central terms of Einstein gravity in AdS
3
is
c=\bar{c}=3\ell/2G
, it is interesting to note here that the limit (
8
) along with the identification of the contraction parameter to the inverse of the AdS radius, leads to

c_{L}=\frac{1}{12}(c-\bar{c})=0,\quad c_{M}=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{12\ell}(c+\bar{c})=\frac{1}{4G}

(9)

for Einstein gravity, as mentioned in the introduction. This can also be obtained from an independent asymptotic symmetry analysis in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes
Barnich:2006av
.

In order to see the ultrarelativistic nature of the limit (
8
), one can look at the Virasoro generators on the cylinder (which also corresponds to the conformal boundary of global AdS
3
spacetime).

\mathcal{L}_{n}=e^{in\omega}\partial_{\omega},\quad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=e^{in\bar{\omega}}\partial_{\bar{\omega}},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\omega,\bar{\omega}=u\pm\phi

The linear combinations required for taking the limit (
8
) means that on the coordinates, the contraction manifests itself as
Bagchi:2012cy

{}\phi\to\phi,u\to\epsilon u\quad\epsilon\to 0.

(10)

In terms of velocities, this means
v/c\sim\sigma/\tau\to\infty
, where
c
is the speed of light, which is the same as a Carrollian or UR limit of
c\to 0
. In terms of the coordinates on the null cylinder
(u,\phi)
, the generators of the BMS algebra take the following form:

L_{n}=ie^{in\phi}(\partial_{\phi}+inu\partial_{u}),\quad M_{n}=ie^{in\phi}\partial_{u}.

(11)

It is of importance to note here that the coordinate
\phi
representing the circle at infinity is compact, whereas the null direction
u
which serves as the time direction in the dual field theory is non-compact. We will be interested in another representation of the BMS algebra where both directions are non-compact. This we will call the “plane” representation. The generator on the plane take the form

L_{n}=-x^{n+1}\partial_{x}-(n+1)x^{n}t\partial_{t},\quad M_{n}=x^{n+1}\partial_{t}.

(12)

It can be checked that these again close to form the (centreless) BMS algebra (
6
). We can map the cylinder to the plane by the coordinate transformation

t=-iue^{i\phi},\quad x=e^{i\phi}

(13)

Again it is straight-forward to check that the generators (
11
) and (
12
) go into each other under this transformation (
13
).

2.2
A brief look at representation theory

In 2d CFTs, states are labelled by the eigenvalues of the
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
operators:

\mathcal{L}_{0}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle=h|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle,\quad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle={\bar{h}}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle.

(14)

Demanding the spectrum to be bounded from below, and noting that
\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
for
n>0
lowers the
(h,{\bar{h}})
values, we are led to the definition of a primary state

\mathcal{L}_{n}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle_{p}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle_{p}=0,\quad\forall n>0.

(15)

A Virasoro highest weight module is built out of these primary states by acting with raising operators. An arbitrary descendant state is constructed by hitting a primary state with a bunch of raising operators
\mathcal{L}_{-n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}
for
n>0
. The primary states form the basis of these highest weight representations and as we mentioned in the introduction, a particular CFT is completely fixed by the spectrum of its primary states and the coefficients associated with the three point functions of these primary states.

Looking at the structure of the BMS algebra (
6
), we could attempt a similar construction of highest weight representations
Bagchi:2009pe
. Now the states would be labelled under
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
.

L_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\Delta|\Delta,\xi\rangle,\quad M_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\xi|\Delta,\xi\rangle.

(16)

Demanding that the spectrum of
\Delta
be bounded from below, we can define a BMS primary
|\Delta,\xi\rangle_{p}
as

L_{n}|\Delta,\xi\rangle_{p}=M_{n}|\Delta,\xi\rangle_{p}=0\quad\forall n>0.

(17)

In a manner analogous to the 2d CFT case, we can build BMS highest weight modules from these primary states by acting with raising operators
L_{-n},M_{-n}
for
n>0
.

It is important to notice that the Virasoro highest weight representation does not contract to the BMS highest weight representation in the UR limit (
8
). To see this directly, we will look at the primary state conditions. We first notice that there is no problem with the labelling of the states in the limit.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle=(h-{\bar{h}})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle\Rightarrow L_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\Delta|\Delta,\xi\rangle,\,\mbox{where}\ \Delta=h-{\bar{h}},

(18a)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle=(h+{\bar{h}})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle\Rightarrow M_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\xi|\Delta,\xi\rangle,\,\mbox{where}\ \xi=\epsilon(h+{\bar{h}}).

(18b)

In the above, the state
|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle
goes over to the state
|\Delta,\xi\rangle
in the limit. Now let us look at the Virasoro primary state conditions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle=(L_{n}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}M_{n})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\,\,\forall\,n>0\Rightarrow M_{n}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=0,\,\,\forall\,n>0

(19a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle=(-L_{-n}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}M_{-n})|h,{\bar{h}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\,\,\forall\,n>0\Rightarrow M_{-n}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=0,\,\,\forall\,n>0.

(19b)

So, Virasoro highest weight representations under the UR contraction become BMS representations with the following characteristics

L_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\Delta|\Delta,\xi\rangle,\,M_{0}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=\xi|\Delta,\xi\rangle,\,M_{n}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=0\ \forall n\neq 0.

(20)

These representations are the so-called induced representations, studied in detail in
Barnich:2014kra
;
Barnich:2015uva
;
Campoleoni:2016vsh
. It should be pretty obvious that these representations are very different from the above described BMS highest weight representations.

The BMS highest weights are reproduced from another limit of the Virasoro which of course again yield the same algebra. This is a non-relativistic (NR) contraction, as opposed to the UR one described earlier. This different contraction is given by
Bagchi:2009pe

L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\quad M_{n}=-\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n})

(21)

The isomorphism between the algebras obtained by these two contractions is a surprising fact in two dimensions
Bagchi:2010zz
. Following this limit on the cylinder Virasoro generators, we can see that the speed of light in this contraction goes to infinity thus justifying the non-relativistic nomenclature. Showing that the highest weights go to highest weights is also a simple exercise.

2.3
BMS modular invariance

In a 2d CFT, the partition function of the theory is defined as

Z_{\mbox{\tiny{CFT}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\mbox{Tr}\left(q^{\mathcal{L}_{0}-c/24}\ \bar{q}^{\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}-\bar{c}/24}\right).

(22)

Here
\tau,\bar{\tau}
are the modular parameters and
q=e^{2\pi i\tau},\,\bar{q}=e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}}
. In a similar vein, we will define the partition function of a 2d BMSFT as

Z_{\mbox{\tiny{BMS}}}(\sigma,\rho)=\mbox{Tr}\left(e^{2\pi i\sigma(L_{0}-c_{L}/24)}\ e^{2\pi i\rho(M_{0}-c_{M}/24)}\right).

(23)

Here the BMS modular parameters are
\sigma,\rho
. We demand that the BMS partition function arises as a smooth limit from the CFT partition function. This necessitates the following identification:

\tau=\sigma+\epsilon\rho,\,\bar{\tau}=\sigma-\epsilon\rho.

(24)

The modular transformation on a 2d CFT reads

\tau\to\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\quad\bar{\tau}\to\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d}

(25)

Following the identification (
24
), we find the BMS version of modular transformations
Bagchi:2013qva
:

\sigma\to\frac{a\sigma+b}{c\sigma+d},\quad\rho\to\frac{\rho}{(c\sigma+d)^{2}}.

(26)

Demanding that the CFT partition function be invariant under modular transformations, particularly the S-transformation

\tau\to-\frac{1}{\tau},

one relates the very high energy spectrum of the theory to the low energy sector and in particular derives an expression for the entropy of states of the theory, given by Cardy’s famous formula

S_{\mbox{\tiny{Cardy}}}=2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{ch}{6}}+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{c}{\bar{h}}}{6}}\right),

(27)

where
h,{\bar{h}}
are conformal weights of the states and
c,\bar{c}
are the central charges of the 2d CFT. Using similar arguments and the BMS S-transformation

\sigma\to-\frac{1}{\sigma},\,\rho\to\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}},

we arrive at the BMS-Cardy formula
Bagchi:2013qva

S_{\mbox{\tiny{BMS-Cardy}}}=2\pi\left(c_{L}\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}}+\Delta\sqrt{\frac{2c_{M}}{\xi}}\right).

(28)

One can also obtain the above formula (
28
) directly from (
27
) by looking carefully at the limit
Riegler:2014bia
;
Fareghbal:2014qga
2
2

2

This crucially involves a minus sign between the two factors in parenthesis in (
27
), a fact which can be attributed to the inner horizon of the original BTZ solution from where the bulk dual FSC is obtained.
or by an exchange of spatial and temporal circles on the BMS torus following methods outlined in
Detournay:2012pc
(see e.g.
Jiang:2017ecm
). The validity of (
28
) can be verified by the fact that this formula correctly reproduces the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy of the putative bulk dual Flatspace Cosmological solutions. We will have more to say about this when we do a bulk calculation in Sec (
8
).

3
BMS one-point function on the torus

Having set the stage, we now move on to our object of interest in the current paper, the torus one-point function of a 2d BMSFT. We expect that in keeping with properties of 2d CFTs, the modular covariance of this quantity, together with crossing symmetry on the sphere, would crucial to ensuring higher point crossing symmetry and higher genus modular invariance in a 2d BMSFT.

3.1
Torus 1-pt function: Definition

For a local primary field
\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)
(where cyl in subscript denotes ‘cylinder’) on the BMS torus with modular parameters
\sigma,\rho
, we define a
BMS torus one-point function
as:

\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}={\rm Tr}\left[\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)e^{-\beta H}e^{i\theta P}\right],

(29)

where
H
is the Hamiltonian which generates translation along the longitude of the torus and
P
generates translation along the meridian (circumference) of the torus. We think of the torus as a cylinder with length
\beta
in which the two ends of the cylinder are glued together. Before gluing, we twist one of the ends by an angle
\theta
.

We will start out by showing that
\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}
is translation invariant. To see this, let us take the basis states to be orthonormal states
|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle
which are simultaneous eigenstates of
H
and
P

H|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle=E_{i}|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle,\,\,\,\,P|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle=P_{i}|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle.

(30)

Then

\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}=\sum_{i}\langle E_{i},P_{i}|\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)e^{-\beta H}e^{i\theta P}|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle.

(31)

Using the fact that
P
and
H
are operators for spatial and temporal translations we have

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u+u^{\prime},\phi+\phi^{\prime})\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle E_{i},P_{i}|e^{iu^{\prime}H}e^{i\phi^{\prime}P}\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)e^{-iu^{\prime}H}e^{-i\phi^{\prime}P}e^{-\beta H}e^{i\theta P}|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle

(32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}e^{iu^{\prime}E_{i}}e^{i\phi^{\prime}P_{i}}e^{-iu^{\prime}E_{i}}e^{-i\phi^{\prime}P_{i}}\langle E_{i},P_{i}|\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)e^{-\beta H}e^{i\theta P}|E_{i},P_{i}\rangle

(33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

Thus,
\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}
is invariant under translation. Using this, we may write

\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}=\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(0,0)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(34)

As we have seen earlier, we can map the cylinder to a plane with coordinates
(t,x)
by

t=-iue^{i\phi},\quad\quad x=e^{i\phi}.

(35)

Since we have built the torus by identifying the ends of a cylinder, this map stays the same.

The operator
H
and
P
can be written in terms of operators
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
on the plane. Then, the one point functions can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}={\rm{Tr}}\left[\phi_{\rm{cyl}}(u,\phi)e^{2\pi i\sigma(L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\rho(M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}\right].

(36)

The primary field in the cylinder
\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)
are related to the primary field on the plane
\phi(x,t)
as

\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)=e^{i\phi\Delta}e^{-iu\xi}\phi(t,x).

(37)

The detailed derivation along with the BMS
3
transformation for primary fields are done in appendix
B
.
Thus the one-point function is given by

\boxed{\langle\phi_{\rm cyl}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}={\rm{Tr}}\left[\phi(0,1)\ e^{2\pi i\sigma(L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\rho(M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}\right].}

(38)

This will be our working definition of the BMS torus one-point function.

3.2
Torus 1-pt function: Modular transformation property

We will now derive the modular transformation properties of a BMS torus one-point function. We will take inspiration from 2d CFTs, like reviewed in Sec. (
2.3
). Modular transformation in relativistic CFT is given by

\tau\rightarrow\gamma.\tau\equiv\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\quad\bar{\tau}\rightarrow\gamma.\bar{\tau}\equiv\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d},

(39)

where
a,b,c,d
are integers satisfying
ad-bc=1
. Under this transformation, the elliptic co-ordinates
(w,\bar{w})
of the torus transforms as

w\rightarrow\gamma.w\equiv\frac{w}{c\tau+d},\quad\bar{w}\rightarrow\gamma.\bar{w}\equiv\frac{\bar{w}}{c\bar{\tau}+d}.

(40)

Thus primary fields transforms as

\displaystyle\phi_{h,\bar{h}}(\gamma.w,\bar{\gamma.w})|_{\gamma.\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial(\gamma.w)}{\partial w}\right)^{-h}\left(\frac{\partial(\gamma.\bar{w})}{\partial\bar{w}}\right)^{-\bar{h}}\phi_{h,\bar{h}}(w,\bar{w})

(41)

Using this and the modular invariance of torus partition function, we can deduce the following transformation property for one point torus function

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{h,\bar{h}}(\gamma.w,\gamma.\bar{w})\rangle_{(\gamma.\tau,\gamma.\bar{\tau})}=(c\tau+d)^{-h}(c\bar{\tau}+d)^{-\bar{h}}\langle\phi_{h,\bar{h}}(w,\bar{w})\rangle_{(\tau,\bar{\tau})}

(42)

To find the transformation property of BMS torus one-point function under BMS modular transformation, we adopt a similar prescription. As stated before, the BMS version of modular transformation reads

\sigma\rightarrow\gamma.\sigma=\frac{a\sigma+b}{c\sigma+d},\quad\quad\rho\rightarrow\gamma.\rho=\frac{\rho}{(c\sigma+d)^{2}}.

(43)

For the coordinate
u
and
\phi
, we can find the analog of (
40
) for by taking its UR limit.
For this, we have to use the contraction

(w,\bar{w})\rightarrow\epsilon u\pm\phi,\quad\quad(\tau,\bar{\tau})\rightarrow\sigma\pm\epsilon\rho.

(44)

We know that

u=\frac{w+\bar{w}}{2\epsilon},\quad\quad\phi=\frac{w-\bar{w}}{2}.

(45)

Thus

\gamma.u=\frac{\gamma.w+\gamma.\bar{w}}{2\epsilon},\quad\quad\tilde{\phi}\equiv\gamma.\phi=\frac{\gamma.w-\gamma.\bar{w}}{2}.

(46)

We expand
\gamma.w
and
\gamma.\bar{w}
in powers of
\epsilon
with the above definitions to obtain:

\displaystyle\gamma.w=\frac{w}{c\tau+d}=\frac{\epsilon u+\phi}{c(\sigma+\epsilon\rho)+d}=\epsilon\left(\frac{u}{c\sigma+d}-\frac{\phi\rho}{(c\sigma+d)^{2}}\right)+\frac{\phi}{c\sigma+d}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}).

(47)

\displaystyle\gamma.\bar{w}=\epsilon\left(\frac{u}{c\sigma+d}-\frac{\phi\rho}{(c\sigma+d)^{2}}\right)-\frac{\phi}{c\sigma+d}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}).

(48)

Thus we have

\gamma.u=\frac{u}{c\sigma+d}-\frac{\phi\rho}{(c\sigma+d)^{2}},\quad\quad\gamma.\phi=\frac{\phi}{c\sigma+d}.

(49)

Note that this is a finite BMS transformation of the form:
u\rightarrow u\partial_{\phi}f(\phi)+g(\phi),\,\phi\rightarrow f(\phi)
.

The BMS primary field with weight
\Delta
and
\xi
transform under

u\rightarrow\gamma.u,\,\phi\rightarrow\gamma.\phi

as

\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(\gamma.u,\gamma.\phi)|_{\gamma.\sigma,\gamma.\rho}=(\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.\phi))^{-\Delta}e^{\xi\frac{\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.u)}{\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.\phi)}}\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(u,\phi)_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(50)

Thus the one point function for BMS primary field would transform as

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(\gamma.u,\gamma.\phi)\rangle_{(\gamma.\sigma,\gamma.\rho)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.\phi))^{-\Delta}e^{\xi\frac{\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.u)}{\partial_{\phi}(\gamma.\phi)}}\langle\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(51)

Substituting (
49
) in the above equation we get

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(\gamma.u,\gamma.\phi)\rangle_{(\gamma.\sigma,\gamma.\rho)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\sigma+d)^{\Delta}e^{-\frac{\xi c\rho}{(c\sigma+d)}}\langle\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(u,\phi)\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(52)

In particular for modular S-transformation
(a=0,b=-1,c=1,d=0)
, we have

\left<\phi_{\Delta,\xi}\right>_{(-1/\sigma,\ \rho/\sigma^{2})}=\sigma^{\Delta}e^{-\frac{\xi\rho}{\sigma}}\langle\phi_{\Delta,\xi}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(53)

We will use the above to derive an asymptotic form of the BMS structure constants in the next section
3
3

3

We note that analogues of the above formulae could have of course been derived completely in the NR limit, with a flip in the time and space directions.
.

4
Asymptotic form of BMS structure constants

In this section, we present two different ways of obtaining the asymptotic form of the BMS structure constants. The first is a saddle-point method, which generalises the BMS-Cardy analysis of e.g.
Bagchi:2012xr
from zero-point functions, i.e. partition functions, to one-point functions on the torus. The second relies on inverting a combination of a Laplace and Fourier transformations and is an improvement on the saddle point analysis.

It is important to state here that the analyses in both methods are independent of basis. We will just assume that the states of the BMSFT are eigenstates of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
, and the forms of the BMS modular transformations discussed in the previous section. So, our analysis should be independent of which of the two representations discussed earlier, the highest weight or the induced, the states of the BMSFT fall into. This is a statement we don’t prove in our paper, as we will not work with induced representations. We will later see that an explicit computation with BMS primaries and highest weight representations, based on the construction of BMS torus blocks yields identical results when the appropriate limits are considered.

The statement about the equivalence of the two representations may seem like an over-reach at this point, but we point the reader to our earlier work on BMS characters in
Bagchi:2019unf
, which remarkably showed that the expressions for characters in these two very different looking representations are actually identical. We expect a similar story to emerge here for the torus one-point function as well. In later sections, we will use the BMS torus blocks to rewrite our torus 1-point function. The leading piece of the expression of the BMS torus blocks in the highest weight representation is again the character. It is not unreasonable to expect that a similar (but admittedly more difficult) calculation of the induced BMS torus block in the same limit may also yield the character as the leading piece. These are one and the same as shown in
Bagchi:2019unf
. We will not have anything to say explicitly about calculations in the induced representation in this work. But the enigmatic equivalence between the two very different representations is something we wish to return to in the near future.

Now, we concentrate on finding the asymptotic form of the BMS structure constants from the modular properties of the one-point function that we derived in the previous section. We first use a saddle-point analysis.

4.1
The saddle-point method

Let us emphasise again that the trace on the left hand side of (
38
) does not depend on the representation which the states of the 2d BMSFT fall into. If we are using a bases
|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
which are both eigenstates of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}

L_{0}|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle=\Delta_{i}|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle,\quad\quad M_{0}|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle=\xi_{i}|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle,

(54)

which is true for both the highest weight and the induced representations, we see that the one-point function for a primary field
\phi_{p}
is given by
4
4

4

The notion of a primary state would of course depend on the representation and definition we are working with stems from the highest weight representation. So this may be a point of dispute. We can choose to define the 1-point function of a torus by (
55
), which will make this a representation independent statement.

\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}=\sum_{i}\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})e^{2\pi i\sigma(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\rho(\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})},

(55)

where
D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})
is the density of states for
|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
. We called
\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
a one-point coefficient and we will use
C_{ipi}
to denote it. Thus

\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}=e^{-2\pi i\left(\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}e^{2\pi i(\sigma\Delta_{i}+\rho\xi_{i})}.

(56)

In this section we will deduce the form of three-point coefficient

C_{ipi}\equiv\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle,

(57)

for large
\Delta_{i}
and
\xi_{i}
. Sometime we will drop the index
i
and use the notation
C_{\Delta p\Delta}\equiv\langle\Delta_{,}\xi|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta,\xi\rangle
.
We define

\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}=e^{2\pi i\left(\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\,\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(58)

Thus from (
56
) and (
58
) we have

\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}=\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}e^{2\pi i(\sigma\Delta_{i}+\rho\xi_{i})},

(59)

The S-modular transformation property of
\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}
can be worked out from that of
\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}
given in (
53
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma},\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\left<\phi_{p}\right>_{(-\frac{1}{\sigma},\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}})}

(60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\sigma^{\Delta_{p}}e^{-\xi_{p}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}}\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\sigma^{\Delta_{p}}e^{-\xi_{p}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}}e^{-2\pi i\left(\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}\,\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}

(62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}\right)}e^{-\xi_{p}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}}\sigma^{\Delta_{p}}\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}.

(63)

Thus the S-modular transformation of
\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}
is given by

\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}=e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi i}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\Delta_{p}\log\sigma\right)}\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma},\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}})}}.

(64)

We invert (
59
) to obtain

\displaystyle D(\Delta,\xi)C_{\Delta p\Delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\sigma d\rho\,e^{-2\pi i(\sigma\Delta+\rho\xi)}\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{(\sigma,\rho)}

(65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\sigma d\rho\,e^{-2\pi i(\sigma\Delta+\rho\xi)}\,e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi i}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\Delta_{p}\log\sigma\right)}\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma},\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}})}}

(66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\sigma d\rho\,\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma},\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}})}}\,\,e^{f(\sigma,\rho)},

(67)

where

f(\sigma,\rho)=2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\sigma\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\rho\frac{c_{M}}{2}+\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi i}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\Delta_{p}\log\sigma-\sigma\Delta-\rho\xi\right).

(68)

For large
(\Delta,\xi)
, we use the saddle point approximation to evaluate the integral (
67
)

D(\Delta,\xi)C_{\Delta p\Delta}\approx\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}},\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}})}}\,e^{f(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})},

(69)

where
\sigma_{c}
and
\rho_{c}
are the critical points of the function
f(\sigma,\rho)
:

\partial_{\sigma}f(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})=0,\,\partial_{\rho}f(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})=0.

These are given by:

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{L}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{c_{L}}{2}+\frac{\rho c_{M}}{\sigma^{3}}-\Delta+\frac{i\Delta_{p}}{2\pi\sigma}+\frac{i\rho\xi_{p}}{2\pi\sigma^{2}}=0,

(70a)

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{M}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi-\frac{i\xi_{p}}{2\pi\sigma}=0.

(70b)

We have two solutions for the above equations. For
(\Delta,\xi)\gg(c_{L},c_{M},\Delta_{p},\xi_{p})
, these are given by

(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})\approx\left(i\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}},i\frac{\left(\xi c_{L}-\Delta c_{M}\right)}{(2\xi)^{3/2}\sqrt{c_{M}}}\right);\quad(\tilde{\sigma}_{c},\tilde{\rho}_{c})\approx\left(-i\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}},-i\frac{\left(\xi c_{L}-\Delta c_{M}\right)}{(2\xi)^{3/2}\sqrt{c_{M}}}\right).

(71)

Among this,
(\sigma_{c},\rho_{c})
is the solution we are interested in. It can be checked that the saddle-point approximation breaks down for the other solution and we will disregard it. For more details of the validity of the saddle-point analysis, we point the interested reader to
Bagchi:2019unf
. Substituting
(\sigma_{{}_{c}},\rho_{c})
in (
69
), we have

D(\Delta,\xi)C_{\Delta p\Delta}\approx\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}},\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}})}}i^{-\Delta_{p}}\left(\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{p}}{2}}e^{2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}c_{L}+\Delta\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}}\right)}e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\xi}+\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)}.

(72)

Now

-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}}=i\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}},\quad\quad\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma_{c}^{2}}=i\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}\left(\frac{\Delta}{\xi}-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)

(73)

So, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}},\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}e^{2\pi i(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}}\Delta_{i}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma_{c}^{2}}\xi_{i})}

(74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}\,e^{-2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}}\Delta_{i}-2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}\left(\frac{\Delta}{\xi}-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)\xi_{i}}.

(75)

In the above equation,
C_{ipi}=\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
is zero when
|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
is the vacuum
|0,0\rangle
. Hence the vacuum would not contribute to the sum on the right hand side of (
75
). Since the exponent is decreasing very fast due to large value of
\Delta
and
\xi
, the leading contribution comes from lightest state in the spectrum, excluding vacuum i.e, state with smallest values of the conformal weights. We denote this state by
|\chi\rangle
and its weights by
(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})
. (Equivalently,
|\chi\rangle=|\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi}\rangle
.) Thus

\widetilde{\langle\phi_{p}\rangle}_{{(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{c}},\frac{\rho_{c}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}})}}\approx D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})C_{\chi p\chi}\,e^{-2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}}\Delta_{\chi}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}\left(-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}+\frac{\Delta}{\xi}\right)\xi_{\chi}\right)}.

(76)

Substituting the above equation in (
72
), we have

\displaystyle D(\Delta,\xi)C_{\Delta p\Delta}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})C_{\chi p\chi}i^{-\Delta_{p}}\left(\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{p}}{2}}e^{2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}c_{L}+\Delta\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}}\right)}e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\xi}+\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)}

(78)

\displaystyle\times\,e^{-2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}}\Delta_{\chi}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}\left(\frac{\Delta}{\xi}-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)\xi_{\chi}\right)}.

Then the three-point coefficient is given by

\displaystyle C_{\Delta p\Delta}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}{D(\Delta,\xi)}C_{\chi p\chi}i^{-\Delta_{p}}\left(\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{p}}{2}}e^{2\pi\left(c_{L}\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}+\Delta\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}}\right)}e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\xi}+\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)}

(79)

\displaystyle\times\,e^{-2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\xi}{c_{M}}}\Delta_{\chi}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}\left(\frac{\Delta}{\xi}-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)\xi_{\chi}\right)}.

The density of states
D(\Delta,\xi)
for large
\Delta
and
\xi
is known (this is basically just the BMS-Cardy formula
Bagchi:2012xr
;
Bagchi:2013qva
;
Bagchi:2019unf
) and given by

D(\Delta,\xi)\approx D(0,0)e^{2\pi\left(c_{L}\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2c_{M}}}+\Delta\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}}{2\xi}}\right)}.

(80)

Substituting this in the previous equation we get

\displaystyle C_{ipi}\approx\frac{D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}{D(0,0)}C_{\chi p\chi}i^{-\Delta_{p}}\left(\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{p}}{2}}e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{\Delta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}+\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)}e^{-2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}}}\Delta_{\chi}+\sqrt{\frac{\xi_{i}}{2c_{M}}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}-\frac{c_{L}}{c_{M}}\right)\xi_{\chi}\right)}.

(81)

This formula is the first of our main results of this paper. In the next subsection, we will improve on this result with a more refined analysis.

For the case when
|\phi_{p}\rangle
and the lightest state
|\chi\rangle
are both scalars, we have
\Delta_{p}=0
and
\Delta_{\chi}=0
. Additionally, for Einstein gravity in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes, we also have
c_{L}=0
in the BMS algebra. For this special case we get

C_{ipi}\equiv\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle\approx\frac{D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}{D(0,0)}C_{\chi p\chi}e^{-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\frac{\Delta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}}e^{-2\pi\frac{\Delta_{i}}{\sqrt{2c_{M}\xi_{i}}}\xi_{\chi}}.

(83)

We would be comparing the dual bulk analysis in Sec. (
8
) to the above formula.

4.2
An improved method

In the previous subsection, we derived an asymptotic form of the one-point coefficient
C_{ipi}\equiv\langle\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}|\phi_{p}(0,1)|\Delta_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle
where we used bases that are simultaneous eigenstates of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
. Here we will obtain the asymptotic formula using a different method that is based on inverting integral transformations. As before, the BMS one-point function is given by

{}\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}=\text{Tr}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi i\sigma(L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\rho(M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}\right)=\sum_{i}D(\Delta,\xi)q^{\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}C_{ipi},

where we have
q=e^{2\pi i\sigma}
and
y=e^{2\pi i\rho}
.
From (
53
),
S
-transformation of
\langle\phi_{p}\rangle
is given as:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}e^{2\pi i\sigma(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\rho(\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}

(84a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{-\Delta_{p}}e^{\xi_{p}\frac{\rho}{\sigma}}\sum_{i}D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})C_{ipi}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{\sigma}(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{2\pi i\frac{\rho}{\sigma^{2}}(\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}.

(84b)

We choose

and consider the limit

. In this limit, the summation in (

) over the discrete
5

\displaystyle T_{p}^{\prime}({\Delta_{i}^{\prime}},{\xi_{i}^{\prime}})e^{-2\pi\beta{\Delta_{i}^{\prime}}}e^{2\pi i\rho{\xi_{i}^{\prime}}}

(88)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=(i\beta)^{-\Delta_{p}}T_{p}(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})e^{2\pi(\beta-\frac{1}{\beta})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\rho\{(1+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}})(\xi_{\chi}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})+\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta}\}},

\displaystyle\quad\times\delta\left[\xi_{i}^{\prime}+(1+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}})(\xi_{\chi}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})+\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta}\right].

Let us denote the two roots of the argument of the

-function as

\displaystyle\beta_{\pm}=\frac{-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}+4(\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\chi})(\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}}{2(\xi_{i}-\frac{c_{M}}{2})}.

(90)

where

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\int_{0}^{\infty}d\Delta_{i}^{\prime}{T}_{p}^{\prime}({\Delta_{i}^{\prime}},{\xi_{i}^{\prime}})e^{-2\pi\beta\Delta_{i}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\star=+,-}\frac{T_{p}(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}{(i\beta_{\star})^{\Delta_{p}}}e^{2\pi(\beta_{\star}-\frac{1}{\beta_{\star}})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}\frac{\delta(\beta-\beta_{\star})}{|\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{\beta_{\star}^{3}}-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta_{\star}^{2}}|}.

(91)

We started out with the assumption that
\beta
is real. Hence, for consistency, we require

to be real.

Now we concentrate on the Laplace transformation. To invert the Laplace-transform in (

-function which is defined for real arguments. Let’s consider the following equivalence of the Cauchy integral theorem and the
\delta
-function (with

being real and

) function such that
\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}F(re^{i\theta})=0
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}dz\frac{F(z)}{z-x_{0}}=F(x_{0})=\int_{-\infty}^{b}dxF(x)\delta(x-x_{0}).

(92)

where
C
is the contour in Figure
2
(relevant for inverse-Laplace transformation) enclosing
x_{0}
where
-\infty<x_{0}<b
.

x
y
\bullet
x_{0}
C_{x=b}
z=b+i\infty
z=b-i\infty
C_{r\rightarrow\infty}

Figure 2:
Contour used for inverting the Laplace transformation

The semicircle part

and by the demanded property of

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}dxF(x)\delta(x-x_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{b-i\infty}^{b+i\infty}dz\frac{F(z)}{z-x_{0}}.

(93)

As
F(z)
is any function belonging to the class of functions having the above specified properties, we conclude that for the problem (and contour) in hand, it is justified to write:

\displaystyle\delta(x-x_{0})\xrightarrow[\text{continuation}]{\text{Analytic}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{z-x_{0}}.

(94)

Inverting the Laplace Laplace-transformation in (

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{{T}_{p}^{\prime}({\Delta_{i}^{\prime}},{\xi_{i}^{\prime}})}{T_{p}(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\star=+,-}\frac{e^{2\pi(\beta_{\star}-\frac{1}{\beta_{\star}})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}}{(i\beta_{\star})^{\Delta_{p}}}\frac{1}{|\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{\beta_{\star}^{3}}-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta_{\star}^{2}}|}\int_{b-i\infty}^{b+i\infty}\frac{d\beta}{i}\frac{e^{2\pi\beta\Delta_{i}^{\prime}}}{2\pi i(\beta-\beta^{*})},

(95)

where,
b>\beta_{+}

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}r\frac{e^{2\pi\Delta_{A}^{\prime}(b+r\cos{\theta}+ir\sin\theta)}}{b+re^{i\theta}-\beta_{\star}}=0

(96)

as along the semi-circle

C_{x=b}
and

. Thus, we can close the contour from the left to get the depicted contour
C
whose semi-circle part doesn’t contribute to the integral. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{{T}_{p}^{\prime}({\Delta_{i}^{\prime}},{\xi_{i}^{\prime}})}{T_{p}(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\star=+,-}(i\beta_{\star})^{-\Delta_{p}}e^{2\pi(\beta_{\star}-\frac{1}{\beta_{\star}})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}\frac{1}{|\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{\beta_{\star}^{3}}-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta_{\star}^{2}}|}\oint_{C}\frac{d\beta}{i}\frac{e^{2\pi\beta\Delta_{i}^{\prime}}}{2\pi i(\beta-\beta^{*})}

(97)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\star=+,-}(i\beta_{\star})^{-\Delta_{p}}e^{2\pi(\beta_{\star}-\frac{1}{\beta_{\star}})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}\frac{e^{2\pi\beta_{\star}\Delta_{i}^{\prime}}}{i|\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{\beta_{\star}^{3}}-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi\beta_{\star}^{2}}|}.

(98)

Thus we note that upon inverting the double-integral transformation in (
88
), the resultant inverse integrand has very sharp (
\delta
-function like) peaks at two different real values
\beta_{\pm}
that are the sole contributors to the inverse transformation (
98
).

To make the initial consideration of
\beta\rightarrow 0^{+}
consistent with the final result,
|\beta_{\pm}|\rightarrow 0^{+}
must be satisfied. This is same as the demand that
|\beta_{\pm}|\ll 1
. We also require
\beta
to be real. Thus, we require
\xi_{i}\gg c_{M}-\xi_{\chi}\pm\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi}
with the assumption of the existence of a
\xi_{\chi}<\frac{c_{M}}{2}
. This implies that

\xi_{i}\gg\frac{c_{M}}{2}\pm\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi}.

In this limit,

\beta_{\pm}\approx-\frac{\xi_{p}}{4\pi\xi_{i}}\pm\sqrt{\frac{{c_{M}}-2\xi_{\chi}}{2\xi_{i}}}.

As
|\beta_{\pm}|\rightarrow 0^{+}
, in each summand in (
98
) the most dominating contribution comes from the factor
e^{2\pi(-\frac{1}{\beta\star})(\Delta_{\chi}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}
. Also, as
\sigma
lies on the upper-half plane, to start with, we had
\beta>0
; hence, it is desirable to have
\beta_{-}
’s contribution to (
98
) negligible in comparison to that of
\beta_{+}
. So, we also need to assume the existence of a
\Delta_{\chi}<\frac{c_{L}}{2}
. Hence, we finally reach

\displaystyle\frac{{T}_{p}({\Delta_{i}},{\xi_{i}})}{T_{p}(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}\approx\frac{(-i)^{\Delta_{p}+1}}{\beta_{+}^{\Delta_{p}-2}}e^{\frac{2\pi}{\beta_{+}}(\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\chi})}\frac{e^{2\pi\beta_{+}(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})}}{\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{\beta_{+}}-\frac{\xi_{p}}{2\pi}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{(-i)^{\Delta_{p}+1}}{2\xi_{i}}{\left(\frac{\xi_{i}}{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\chi}}\right)}^{\frac{\Delta_{p}-1}{2}}e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}{\xi_{i}}+\frac{\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\chi}}{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\chi}}\right)+2\pi[\sqrt{\frac{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\chi}}{\xi_{i}}}(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2})+\sqrt{\frac{\xi_{i}}{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\chi}}}(\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\chi})]}.

Density of states
: Let us call the lightest primary field in the theory
\mathcal{O}
. This may be the vacuum, but we are further generalising our results by considering cases where it may not be so and
\Delta_{\chi}\geqslant\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}\geqslant 0
. Taking a cue out of Liouville theory, a BMS version of Liouville theory could be amenable to such a generalisation. We assume the existence of
\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}<\frac{c_{L}}{2}
and
\xi_{\mathcal{O}}<\frac{c_{M}}{2}
. Clearly, putting
\Delta_{p}=0=\xi_{p}
directly in (
) and replacing the primary field
\chi
by
\mathcal{O}
, we get the asymptotic formula for density of states in the large
\xi_{i}
limit:

\displaystyle\frac{D(\Delta_{i},\xi_{i})}{D(\Delta_{\mathcal{O}},\xi_{\mathcal{O}})}=\frac{-i}{2\xi_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}{\xi_{i}}}\exp 2\pi\left({\sqrt{\frac{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}{\xi_{i}}}\left(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\xi_{i}}{\frac{c_{M}}{2}-\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}}\left(\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}\right)}\right).

(100)

Substituting (
100
) in (
), we find an asymptotic formula for the 3-point coefficient
C_{ipi}
in the large
\xi_{i}
limit:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptC_{ipi}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle(-i)^{\Delta_{p}}C_{\chi p\chi}\frac{D(\Delta_{\chi},\xi_{\chi})}{D(\Delta_{\mathcal{O}},\xi_{\mathcal{O}})}\left(\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{p}-1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}-2\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}}\,e^{\frac{\xi_{p}}{2}\left(-\frac{2\Delta_{i}-c_{L}}{2\xi_{i}}+\frac{c_{L}-2\Delta_{\chi}}{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}\right)}

(101)

\displaystyle\times e^{2\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}}\left(\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\chi}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{i}}{c_{M}-2\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}}\left(\frac{c_{L}}{2}-\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}\right)+\left(\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\chi}}{2\xi_{i}}}-\sqrt{\frac{c_{M}-2\xi_{\mathcal{O}}}{2\xi_{i}}}\,\right)\left(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}\right)\right)}.

Note that the above asymptotic formula is valid irrespective of the order of magnitude of
\frac{\Delta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}
. Furthermore, if we take the field
(\Delta_{\mathcal{O}},\xi_{\mathcal{O}})
to be the vacuum and consider the limit in which
c_{M}\gg\xi_{\chi}
, then we get back the asymptotic formula (
81
) obtained in the previous section using a saddle-point analysis. So, in this sense, the range of validity of the above formula is broader than that of (
81
).

5
Machinery for BMS highest weights

We have, in the preceding section, derived the asymptotic form of the BMS torus one-point function using general analyses, that did not rely on the explicit representations the states of the BMSFT fall into, except for the fact that the states were simultaneous eigenstates of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
. We now want to concentrate on BMS highest weight representations and use this to find the expression for the torus one-point function of primary fields. In this section and the next, we will build machinery for our ultimate calculation, which will be described in Sec (
7
).

5.1
BMS highest weight modules

As a basis for calculating the trace in the partition function or the one-point function on the torus (
38
), we can use the BMS primary states and their descendants. As we have briefly reviewed in Sec. 2, the primary states are eigenstates of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}

L_{0}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle=\Delta_{A}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\quad\quad M_{0}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle=\xi_{A}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,

(102)

which are annihilated by
L_{n}
and
M_{n}
for
n>0

L_{n}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle=0,\quad\quad M_{n}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle=0,\quad n>0.

(103)

By acting the operator
L_{-n}
and
M_{-n}
on the primary states we could raise the eigenvalue of
L_{0}
. Such states which are obtained by acting a series of
L_{-n}
and
M_{-n}
operators on the primary states are called descendant states. A BMS module of a primary state
|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle
is the set which contain the primary states and all its descendant. States in the module have the form

L_{-1}^{k_{1}}L_{-2}^{k_{2}}....L_{-l}^{k_{l}}M_{-1}^{q_{1}}M_{-2}^{q_{2}}....M_{-r}^{q_{r}}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\equiv L_{\overrightarrow{k}}M_{\overrightarrow{q}}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,

(104)

where
\overrightarrow{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},....,k_{l})
and
\overrightarrow{q}=(q_{1},q_{2},....,q_{r})
and its
L_{0}
eigenvalue is given by

L_{0}L_{\overrightarrow{k}}M_{\overrightarrow{q}}|\Delta,\xi\rangle=(N+\Delta_{A})L_{\overrightarrow{k}}M_{\overrightarrow{q}}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\quad\mbox{where}\ N=\sum_{i}ik_{i}+\sum_{j}jq_{j}.

(105)

N
is called the level of the state. To simplify or shorten equations, we will sometimes use the shorthand notation
|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\equiv|A\rangle
.

We can group the states according to their level because inner product of sates having different level is zero. In general we can write the states at level
N
as

|\Psi\rangle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N}(L_{-j})^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N}(M_{-j})^{k_{j}}\right)|A\rangle,

(106)

with
\sum_{j}jN_{j}=N
. In the table below we shown the basis states for up to level 3.

Level

States

N=0

|\Psi_{1}\rangle=|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle

N=1

|\Psi_{1}\rangle=L_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{2}\rangle=M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle

N=2

\begin{array}[]{l}|\Psi_{1}\rangle=L_{-1}^{2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{2}\rangle=L_{-2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{3}\rangle=L_{-1}M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\\
|\Psi_{4}\rangle=M_{-2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{5}\rangle=M_{-1}^{2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\end{array}

N=3

\begin{array}[]{l}|\Psi_{1}\rangle=L_{-1}^{3}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{2}\rangle=L_{-1}L_{-2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{3}\rangle=L_{-1}^{2}M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\\
|\Psi_{4}\rangle=L_{-3}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{5}\rangle=L_{-2}M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{6}\rangle=L_{-1}M_{-2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\\
|\Psi_{7}\rangle=M_{-3}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{8}\rangle=L_{-1}M_{-1}^{2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\,\,\,|\Psi_{9}\rangle=M_{-1}M_{-2}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\\
|\Psi_{10}\rangle=M_{-1}^{3}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\end{array}

Table 1:
States in the BMS module upto level 3

We will use
|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A};N,i\rangle
to denote the
i
-th basis state at level
N
. Sometimes, we will simply this further as
|A;N,i\rangle
.

Gram matrix and their inverse

For a given module we can form a matrix by taking the inner product of the basis states. This is known as the Gram matrix. Since states in different levels are orthogonal to each other, the Gram matrix will be block diagonal with each block for each level. We use the notation
K^{(N)}
for the Gram matrix at level
N
. Since we have two basis states at level 2, the The Gram matrix for level 1 will be a
2\times 2
matrix

\displaystyle K^{(1)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\langle\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|L_{1}L_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle&\langle\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|L_{1}M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\\
\langle\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|M_{1}L_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle&\langle\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|M_{1}M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}2\Delta_{A}&2\xi_{A}\\
2\xi_{A}&0\end{array}\right].

The Gram matrix
K^{(2)}
for level 2 is a
5\times 5
matrix given by

\displaystyle K^{(2)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}4\Delta_{A}(2\Delta_{A}+1)&6\Delta_{A}&4\xi_{A}(2\Delta_{A}+1)&6\xi_{A}&8\xi^{2}_{A}\\
6\Delta_{A}&4\Delta_{A}+6c_{L}&6\xi_{A}&4\xi_{A}+6c_{M}&0\\
4\xi_{A}(2\Delta_{A}+1)&6\xi_{A}&4\xi^{2}_{A}&0&0\\
6\xi_{A}&4\xi_{A}+6c_{M}&0&0&0\\
8\xi^{2}_{A}&0&0&0&0\end{array}\right].

Note that the Gram matrix has a peculiar triangular structure in which all the matrix elements below the anti-diagonal elements are zero. This is due to way we order our basis
Bagchi:2019unf
. In fact for any level we can order our basis states such that the Gram matrix will have the following form:

K^{(N=\text{odd})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}K_{1,1}&&\cdots&&A_{\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N}}\\
&&&\iddots&0\\
\vdots&&\iddots&&\vdots\\
&A_{2}\\
A_{1}&0&\cdots&&0\end{array}\right),

(117)

K^{(N=\text{even})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccc}K_{1,1}&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&A_{\widetilde{{\rm dim}}_{N}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&&&&&&&\iddots&0\\
&&\ddots&&&&&A_{k+l}&&\vdots\\
&&&D_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&&&0&D_{2}&\cdots&0&&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&&&\vdots\\
&&&0&0&\cdots&D_{l}\\
&&A_{k}&&&&&0\\
\vdots&\iddots&&&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
A_{1}&0&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&0\end{array}\right).

(118)

In the above, the non-zero Gram matrix in the anti-diagonal line, labelled by
A
, and the diagonal elements labelled
D
which appear for even
N
are of the form

\langle A|(\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}})(\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}})(\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}})(\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}})|A\rangle,

(119)

where
\sum_{j=1}^{N}jN_{j}=N
. These are the matrix elements for which the number of
L
-operators and number of
M
-operators in the inner product are equal. In particular, the elements of
D
are the norm of self conjugate states
(\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}})(\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{N_{j}})|A\rangle
(states which looks the same when
L
and
M
are exchanged, for example, state like
L_{-1}M_{-1}|A\rangle
). For more details about the above structure, we refer the interested reader to
Bagchi:2019unf
.

In a later section, we would like to take the large
\xi_{A}
limit of the BMS torus blocks (which are of course to be defined later). So, let us point out some important properties of the matrix elements of the form given above. In explicitly calculating the matrix elements, we make use of the BMS algebra. The highest power in
\xi_{A}
comes from the term is which each
L_{j}
contracted with
M_{-j}
and each
M_{j}
contracted with
L_{-j}
to form
M_{0}
which will then act on the vacuum to give
\xi_{A}
. Thus the highest power in
\xi_{A}
is equal to the number of
L
-operators or
M
-operators inside the inner product. This is given by
\sum_{i=1}^{N}N_{j}
.
We also note down the following factorization property of the inner product

\displaystyle\langle A|\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\langle A|M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}|A\rangle\langle A|L_{j}^{k_{j}}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle.

(120)

The terms in the above equation are given by

\displaystyle\langle A|M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}|A\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(N_{j}-k_{j})!\langle A|[M_{j},L_{-j}]|A\rangle=(N_{j}-k_{j})!(\xi_{A}-c_{L}(j^{3}-j)),

\displaystyle\langle A|L_{j}^{k_{j}}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{j}!(\xi_{A}-c_{L}(j^{3}-j)),

(122)

where the factorial account for the number of ways to contracts the
L
and
M
operators.
From these properties we can deduce another factorisation formula which we will use later. Before we present a formula for generic matrix elements, we demonstrate it with the following example

\displaystyle\langle A|M_{2}M_{1}^{3}L_{2}^{2}L_{-1}^{3}L_{-2}M_{-2}^{2}|A\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\langle A|M_{1}L_{-1}|A\rangle\langle A|M_{2}M_{1}^{2}L_{2}^{2}L_{-1}^{2}L_{-2}M_{-2}^{2}|A\rangle

(123)

\displaystyle\langle A|M_{2}M_{1}^{3}L_{2}^{2}L_{-1}^{3}L_{-2}M_{-2}^{2}|A\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle A|M_{2}L_{-2}|A\rangle\langle A|M_{1}^{3}L_{2}^{2}L_{-1}^{3}M_{-2}^{2}|A\rangle

(124)

\displaystyle\langle A|M_{2}M_{1}^{3}L_{2}^{2}L_{-1}^{3}L_{-2}M_{-2}^{2}|A\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\langle A|L_{2}M_{-2}|A\rangle\langle A|M_{2}M_{1}^{3}L_{2}L_{-1}^{3}L_{-2}M_{-2}|A\rangle.

(125)

In general we have

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\frac{\langle A|\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}-\delta_{j,r}}\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}-\delta_{j,r}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle}{\langle A|\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle}=\frac{(N_{r}-k_{r})^{-1}}{\langle A|M_{r}L_{-r}|A\rangle}.

(126)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\frac{\langle A|\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}-\delta_{j,r}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}-\delta_{j,r}}|A\rangle}{\langle A|\prod_{j=N}^{1}M_{j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=N}^{1}L_{j}^{k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}L_{-j}^{N_{j}-k_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}M_{-j}^{k_{j}}|A\rangle}=\frac{1}{k_{r}\langle A|M_{r}L_{-r}|A\rangle}.

(127)

We use
K_{(N)}
to denote the inverse of the Gram matrix
K^{(N)}
and specify the matrix elements by
K^{ab}_{(N)}
. Due to the structure of the matrix given above, the inverse Gram matrix
K_{(N)}
will have the following structure:

K_{(N=\text{odd})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&\cdots&0&\cdots&\frac{1}{A_{\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N}}}\\
0&&&\iddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\iddots&&\vdots\\
&\frac{1}{A_{2}}\\
\frac{1}{A_{1}}&\iddots&\cdots&&K^{\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N},\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N}}\end{array}\right),

(128)

K_{(N=\text{even})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccc}0&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots&&0&&\cdots&\frac{1}{A_{\widetilde{{\rm dim}}_{N}}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&&&&&&&\iddots&\\
&&\ddots&&&&&\frac{1}{A_{k+l}}&&\vdots\\
&&&\frac{1}{D_{1}}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&&&0&\frac{1}{D_{2}}&\cdots&0&&&\vdots\\
0&&&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&&&\vdots\\
&&&0&0&\cdots&\frac{1}{D_{l}}\\
&&\frac{1}{A_{k}}&&&&&\ddots\\
\vdots&\iddots&&&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{A_{1}}&&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&&\cdots&K^{\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N},\widetilde{\rm dim}_{N}}\end{array}\right).

(129)

Note that for matrix elements in the anti-diagonal line as well as the diagonal elements of the matrix at the centre for even level,
K_{(N)}^{ab}
are simply given by
1/{K^{(N)}_{ab}}
. This fact will be important when we study large
\xi_{A}
limit of the BMS torus blocks. Even for non-zero, non-anti-diagonal elements
K_{(N)}^{ab}
, the leading term in
\xi_{A}
is given by
1/K^{(N)}_{ab}
.

5.2
BMS mode expansion

Inspired from 2d CFT, we propose that a local field
\phi_{p}(t,x)
with dimensions
(\Delta,\xi)
in a 2d BMSFT on the 2d Euclidean plane (endowed with a degenerate metric) has the following mode-expansion

\displaystyle\phi_{p}(t,x)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{N}}{x}^{-\Delta-m}e^{\frac{t}{x}\xi}(-1)^{n}{\left(\frac{t}{x}\right)}^{-n}\phi_{m,n},

(130)

where the quasi-primary field satisfies the following Hermitian conjugation relation

\displaystyle\phi_{p}^{\dagger}(t,x)=x^{-2\Delta}e^{2\xi\frac{t}{x}}\phi_{p}\left(\frac{1}{x},\frac{t}{x^{2}}\right).

(131)

It is interesting to note here the
n
index in (
130
) runs over negative integers only. This condition is imposed on
n
to prevent blowing up of
\phi_{p}
at
(t=0,x=1)
which is mapped to the point
(0,0)
on the cylinder.
Putting the mode-expansion ansatz into the above conjugation equation, we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{N}}x^{-\Delta-m}e^{\xi\frac{t}{x}}(-1)^{n}{\left(\frac{t}{x}\right)}^{-n}\phi_{m,n}^{\dagger}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{N}}{x}^{-\Delta+m}e^{\xi\frac{t}{x}}(-1)^{n}{\left(\frac{t}{x}\right)}^{-n}\phi_{m,n}.

(132)

Comparing the coefficients on the both sides, we conclude that the BMS quasi-primary modes satisfy the following Hermitian-conjugation condition:

\displaystyle\phi_{m,n}^{\dagger}=\phi_{-m,n}

(133)

The asymptotic ‘in’-state
|\Delta,\xi\rangle
is created from the vacuum
|0\rangle
of the theory as below:

\displaystyle|\Delta,\xi\rangle\equiv\lim_{x\to 0}\lim_{\frac{t}{x}\to 1}\phi_{p}(t,x)|0\rangle.

(134)

Clearly, for the ‘in’ states to be well-defined, the vacuum must satisfy

\displaystyle\phi_{m,n}|0\rangle=0\text{ , for $m>-\Delta$}\Longleftrightarrow\langle 0|\phi_{m,n}=0\text{ , for $m<\Delta$}.

(135)

From the plane-representation of
BMS_{3}
algebra, we know that for a local primary field
\phi_{p}
on 2d Euclidean plane (for
n\geqslant-1
)

\displaystyle[L_{n},\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(t,x)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[x^{n+1}\partial_{x}+(n+1)x^{n}t\partial_{t}+(n+1)(\Delta x^{n}-n\xi x^{n-1}t)]\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(t,x)

(136)

\displaystyle[M_{n},\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(t,x)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[-x^{n+1}\partial_{t}+(n+1)\xi x^{n}]\phi_{\Delta,\xi}(t,x).

(137)

Substituting the mode-expansion ansatz into the above representation and comparing the coefficients on both sides, we obtain the following mode-commutation relations (for
n\geqslant-1
) only for primary fields:

\displaystyle[L_{n},\phi_{j,k}]=[n(\Delta-1-k)-j]\phi_{j+n,k}+n^{2}\xi\phi_{j+n,k+1},\quad\forall k\leqslant-1,

\displaystyle[L_{n},\phi_{j,0}]=[n(\Delta-1)-j]\phi_{j+n,0},

\displaystyle[M_{n},\phi_{j,k}]=n\xi\phi_{j+n,k}+(k-1)\phi_{j+n,k-1},\quad\forall k\leqslant 0.

(138)

Although we shall not be highlighting this in the main text, most of the intrinsic analysis of 2d BMSFT done in this paper, and elaborated in the appendices, would be done with the help of the mode expansions defined in this section.

6
BMS Torus blocks

In this section, using the tools we have developed for the BMS highest weight states, we construct the torus blocks for 2d BMSFTs. We start by defining these blocks. We then go onto calculating the contribution for low orders and then work in a particular limit, which we will call the large
\xi_{A}
limit, which will be crucial for our analysis of the one-point functions in the following section.

6.1
Highest weight torus blocks

For our basis we can use the primary states and their descendants. Then taking the trace with respect to this basis in (
38
), the one-point function for primary field
\phi_{p}
is given by

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{(\sigma,\rho)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi i\sigma(L_{0}-c_{L}/2)}e^{2\pi i\rho(M_{0}-c_{M}/2)}\right)

(139)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\pi i(\sigma c_{L}/2+\rho c_{M}/2)}\sum_{a,b}K^{ab}\langle\Psi_{a}|\phi_{p}(0,1)\,e^{2\pi i\sigma L_{0}}e^{2\pi i\rho M_{0}}|\Psi_{b}\rangle,

(140)

where
|\Psi_{a}\rangle
are the primary states and their descendant.
K^{ab}
is the element of the inverse Gram Matrix formed by taking inner products of the basis states. Since inner products between states in different modules vanish, we can separate out the contribution of each module
|\Delta,\xi\rangle
to the one-point function. So, with a bit of hindsight, we may write the one point function as

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{p}\rangle_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\pi i(\sigma c_{L}+\rho c_{M})}\sum_{A}D(\Delta_{A},\xi_{A})e^{2\pi i\sigma\Delta_{A}}e^{2\pi i\rho\xi_{A}}\langle A|\phi_{p}(0,1)|A\rangle\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho),

(141)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle e^{-\pi i(\sigma c_{L}+\rho c_{M})}\sum_{A}D(\Delta_{A},\xi_{A})e^{2\pi i\sigma\Delta_{A}}e^{2\pi i\rho\xi_{A}}C_{ApA}\,\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho),

(142)

where
\{A\}
is the collection of all the the primary states and
D(\Delta_{A},\xi_{A})
is the number of primary states (density of states, or, multiplicity) with dimension
(\Delta_{A},\xi_{A})
. As before,
C_{ApA}\equiv\langle A|\phi_{p}(0,1)|A\rangle
is the three point coefficient. We called
\mathcal{F}
the BMS torus block. This is completely determined by BMS symmetry alone.

To simplify our notation, let us use
q=e^{2\pi i\sigma}
and
y=e^{2\pi i\rho}
. Then, the BMS torus block
\mathcal{F}
is formally given by

\displaystyle{\boxed{\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho)=\frac{{\rm Tr}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)q^{L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}\right)}{q^{\Delta_{A}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{\xi_{A}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}C_{ApA}}.}}

(143)

The trace,
{\rm Tr}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}}
, is over the states in the module of
|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle
. Thus we have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)q^{L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}\right)=\sum_{N;i,j}K^{ij}_{(N)}\langle A,N,i|\phi_{p}\ q^{L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}|A,N,j\rangle.

(144)

For a state at level
N

L_{0}|A;N,i\rangle=(N+\Delta_{A})|A;N,i\rangle\Rightarrow q^{L_{0}}|A;N,i\rangle=q^{N+\Delta_{A}}|A;N,i\rangle.

(145)

Using this in (
144
), we get

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left(\phi_{p}(0,1)q^{L_{0}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{M_{0}-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}\right)=\sum_{N}q^{N+\Delta_{A}-\frac{c_{L}}{2}}y^{-\frac{c_{M}}{2}}\sum_{i,j}K^{ij}_{(N)}\langle A;N,i|\phi_{p}y^{M_{0}}|A;N,j\rangle.

(146)

Further substituting this in (
143
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\Delta_{A},\xi_{A},c_{L},c_{M}}^{\Delta_{p},\xi_{p}}(\sigma,\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{N}q^{N}\sum_{i,j}K^{ij}_{(N)}\frac{\langle A;N,i|\phi_{p}(0,1)y^{M_{0}}|A;N,j\rangle}{y^{\xi_{A}}C_{ApA}}

(147)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{N}q^{N}\mathcal{F}_{N}(\Delta_{p},\xi_{p};\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|c_{L},c_{M}|\rho),

(148)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}(\Delta_{p},\xi_{p};\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|c_{L},c_{M}|\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{y^{\xi_{A}}C_{ApA}}\sum_{i,j}K^{ij}_{(N)}\langle A;N,i|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|A;N,j\rangle.

(149)

From (
148
) can see that
q^{N}\mathcal{F}_{N}
is the contribution of level
N
to the torus BMS block. In what follows, we give an explicit expression for the BMS torus block for the first couple of levels.

\mathcal{F}_{N}
for level 1

As an example let us calculate
\mathcal{F}_{N}
for level 1.
For this level, the states are

|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A};1,1\rangle\equiv|\Psi_{1}\rangle=L_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,\quad\quad\quad|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A};1,2\rangle\equiv|\Psi_{2}\rangle=M_{-1}|\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}\rangle,

(150)

and the Gram matrix is given in (
5
).
The inverse of the Gram matrix is

\displaystyle K_{(1)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\frac{1}{2\xi_{A}}\\
\frac{1}{2\xi_{A}}&-\frac{\Delta_{A}}{2\xi^{2}_{A}}\\
\end{array}\right].

Now,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Delta_{p},\xi_{p};\Delta_{A},\xi_{A}|c_{L},c_{M}|\rho)

(153)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{y^{\xi_{A}}C_{ApA}}\left(K^{11}_{(1)}\langle\Psi_{1}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{1}\rangle+K^{12}_{(1)}\langle\Psi_{1}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{2}\rangle\right.

(155)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\left.+K^{21}_{(1)}\langle\Psi_{2}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{1}\rangle+K^{22}_{(1)}\langle\Psi_{2}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{2}\rangle\right).

Since
K^{11}_{(1)}
is zero, we don’t have to calculate
\langle\Psi_{1}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{1}\rangle
. The other three matrix elements are listed below

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{ApA}\,y^{\xi_{A}}(\xi_{p}\Delta_{p}-\xi_{p}+2\xi_{A}),

(156)

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{2}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y^{\xi_{A}}C_{ApA}(\xi_{p}\Delta_{p}-\xi_{p}+2\xi_{A}+2\pi i\rho\xi_{p}^{2}),

(157)

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{2}|\phi_{p}(0,1)e^{2\pi iM_{0}}|\Psi_{2}\rangle
